Blood High School (BHS) — IP Proposal

Working document · each section comes with a guide note explaining its role
※ This proposal was written in collaboration with an AI assistant (Claude), based on the author's own materials and intent.
FROM THE AUTHOR
A Word from the Author

Hello. I am the author of Blood High School.

Blood High School is a "newtro" school fantasy that reweaves, for today, the feel of Japanese shonen anime from the late 1990s through the 2000s and up to around 2010. Before you read the proposal, I would like to tell you why I came to make this work.

As a teenager I watched an enormous amount of Japanese anime from that era. Those shows were childlike and noisy, and yet at some point, as you kept watching, you found yourself facing the dark side of the world. Many of them were philosophical stories, something close to a hymn to humanity. The heroes were kind and righteous, and audiences treated it as a virtue when they held on to their humanity even in front of trials they could not overcome.

But I feel that works like that have become hard to make again. That sensibility was possible because it was that era. Today's works have a very different texture, and to me, kind and righteous protagonists no longer seem to be drawn as attractively as they once were.

So I am gathering, one by one, the things I loved in the works of that era and making them myself. In a sense, Blood High School is also a work I am making for myself.

And I hope that those who read it will feel the romance of that era along with me.

What follows is the proposal, which lays out the structure and potential of the work on the basis of that feeling.

PART 02

Concept — Why This Work

The producer's question: "There are plenty of similar titles already. Why should we do this one?" — The box that explains which empty spot in the market this work occupies. It shows the work's positioning, not how fun it is. It has to explain why the core code "newtro" works in this market right now. The work's own points of appeal (the ones the author was aiming for) can be worked in here as well. But it must be written in the language of "why this sells," not "I like this."

Where the Work Began

This work began with a simple thirst. There is clearly a readership that wants to experience once more the particular rhythm of 2000s Japanese anime — direction that moves between light and heavy, the character visuals of that era, and a self-aware handling of clichés. Yet in today's Korean, English-language and Chinese-language subculture markets alike, there are almost no new titles that aim squarely at that thirst. As the first reader of that empty spot, the author took their own thirst as the market's thirst and planned this work accordingly.

Why It Works in This Market Now

The "newtro" of this work is not a simple replay of nostalgia but a reconstruction of filtered nostalgia. It keeps the emotional coordinates unique to the old works (rhythm, visuals, the handling of clichés) while deliberately removing and reassembling the elements that grate on a contemporary sensibility (unconditional family love imposed on the characters, unnecessary cruelty, sagging plots). There is a readership that wants to rewatch "that show from back then," only to find, on opening it, that too much of it no longer matches today's sensibility to get through to the end — this work aims to fill exactly that gap.

This positioning competes directly neither with the mainstream of Korean web novels (regression, possession and overpowered-protagonist stories) nor with the mainstream of English-language web fiction (progression fantasy and LitRPG — fantasy built around game systems). It sits at coordinates of its own.

Defining the Core Code "Newtro"

"Newtro," as this work uses the word, means consciously reconstructing, from today's vantage point, the grammar of Japanese shonen anime from 1990 to 2008 (or to around 2010).

It is not simple retro flavor; it is a way of making the genre grammar of that era itself run again.

Deliberate Hybridity of Language

The text is written almost entirely in Korean, but vocabulary peculiar to Japanese anime is woven naturally into the characters' speech and the narration: "ittekimasu," "boku," "ore-sama," "shizuka ni," "yoroshiku," "yurusanai," and so on. Through this code the reader lands at once on the emotional coordinates of "that genre, in that era." For readers who do not know the code, it works as a distinctive prose style and sets the work apart.

This Japanese code also serves as a device for telling the characters' voices apart. Kuro (the model student: standard speech), Eunho (sly Japanese slang), Miko (a proud young lady's speech), Aoi (Kansai dialect) — in an ensemble story with many characters, who is speaking can be identified instantly from the manner of speech alone, and the distinction between each character's tone survives intact in Japanese translation as well.

The device works just the same in English. The English–Japanese hybrid edition melts the same Japanese vocabulary into the English text in romanized form ("Ittekimasu," "Ore-sama," "sensei," etc.), recreating in prose the experience of overseas fans in the 2000s who first met Japanese anime through fansubs sprinkled with untranslated honorifics and interjections. For Korean and English-language readers, this hybrid style is itself a memory of that era — one more newtro device. The distinction between the characters' voices is kept in this edition too. For readers unfamiliar with the hybrid style, a Pure English edition with the Japanese borrowings removed is offered alongside it.

The same device works in the Chinese-speaking world. Its anime fan culture ("erciyuan," 二次元) is used to a way of speaking that mixes in Japanese words rendered by sound in Chinese characters (米娜桑, 哦哈哟, 卡哇伊, etc.) and to borrowed honorifics such as "~酱, ~君, ~様," and nostalgia for the fansub groups (字幕组) of the 2000s remains as well. The Chinese–Japanese hybrid edition is written in this manner of speech, and a Pure Chinese edition for general readers is offered alongside it. Both editions are available in Simplified and Traditional Chinese. The Traditional edition is not just the Simplified text with the characters converted; its vocabulary and sound-based renderings were separately refined so that readers of Traditional Chinese can read it comfortably.

Self-Aware Use of Clichés

A little-boy principal, a beautiful student council president who holds more power than that principal, an "ore-sama" rival, a tournament, tsukkomi-and-boke comedy pairings, the childhood friend who is really a spirit, a family tragedy — all are staples of "that era," but the work uses them knowing that it is using them. Self-aware descriptions such as the "kime pose" (signature pose) are typical, as is the passage in Episode 9 where Herbaeken says, "The culture festival has the legend of the band club... the legend of Japanese anime, where the band club always has an accident right before the show and the protagonist steps in as a substitute." Using clichés as tools while sharing that fact with the reader — this self-awareness produces both the work's comedic rhythm and its emotional density.

A Visual Code That Carries Its Era

The most immediate entry point of newtro is the eye. Before opening the text, a reader judges a work's era and genre in zero seconds from the first impression of its cover, illustrations and character designs. The character visuals of this work deliberately borrow what 2000s Japanese school and dark-fantasy anime built up — gothic and stylized beauty, exaggerated hair silhouettes, and a wealth of transformation signs such as costume variations. This gets readers past the entry barrier fastest, and it is also a key device for securing recognition in advance across IP extension lines such as merchandise, art books and fan works.

SIGN. The Author's Signature For those who want to dig deeper — the core of the work and its potential as an IP are packed most densely here. Dig deeper ▼

The Author's Signature — The Message Only This Work Carries

If newtro is the work's formal code, the following three threads are the author's own message, borrowed from no older work. They are what make this a contemporary work rather than a mere recreation of nostalgia.

A"What if ordinary me were given power?" — a hero of emptying and descent, not an idol.

Shonen protagonists usually go up. From weak to strong, from the bottom to the top — and with the "overpowered regressor" of today's web novels, the catharsis of that ascent becomes even more intense. There is a clear appeal to a figure who, from the start or soon after, stands complete at the center of the world and saves everyone. The coordinates this work chose, however, are on the opposite side — because the author wanted, rather than a completed protagonist one can only look up to, a protagonist readers can lay themselves over and agonize alongside.

Kuro at the starting point is an ordinary, gentle boy whose own world is still empty. He empathizes deeply — at times naively — with other people's pain, but he does not yet have the firm judgment of his own to support that empathy. He hardly knows how to doubt, nor does he set himself up as an absolute standard. His strength lies not in overwhelming power but in the seeds of the delicacy and the willingness to agonize that are yet to be formed.

That emptiness is soon put to the test. Holding overwhelming power and facing despair too great to bear, the still-unformed Kuro is at one point swept away by that power and despair — to the point of being seized by the very impulse of the arrogant savior that the ascending hero so often enshrines: to end everything in the name of "saving" everyone from suffering. This work portrays that not as a heroic awakening but as the most dangerous catastrophe. Had he been a completed hero from the start, the work would have done no more than set up one more idol.

Only after the empty boy has gone through every step of wavering and falling does Kuro finally reach the firmness to judge for himself and shoulder responsibility. Instead of unconditional reverence within the frame of "family," he tells right from wrong; he pities someone and still says no to what is wrong; and he takes on what he believes to be right even at the cost of looking foolish or being hated. Not the decision of a hero to be looked up to, but one person's responsibility — a firmness that becomes real only because he went through that process.

Seen as a curve, the hero shaped this way points not up but down. Where a typical protagonist draws a rising curve, Kuro and Eunho come down in the opposite direction. Kuro goes from an ordinary student to a family head who takes on the family's sins and karma — outwardly a promotion, but in truth a descent into a heavier burden. Eunho is a figure who voluntarily came down from the seat of a spirit and has lived for many years as a human boy — an unmistakable, voluntary descent.

This structure touches two archetypes of the religious hero. The bodhisattva (菩薩) of Buddhism postpones his own enlightenment and comes down to the human world to remain beside living beings, and the kenosis (κένωσις, self-emptying) of Christianity empties itself of divine glory and takes on human form. In both traditions, "a voluntary emptying that comes down from above" functions as the mark of true heroism. It means that the hero of this work stands at coordinates directly opposite the desire for ascent in today's web novels, where "the overpowered regressor goes up." Moreover, Kuro's power awakens not through the awakening of an isolated self but through his connection with those who stand beside him — the moments when he fights not for himself but for the people he must protect form the emotional peak of the work, and it functions as a narrative of "growing together."

In the end, this hero does not make readers look up; it makes them walk alongside. That is because it hands the question "What would I have done if I had been given that power?" back to readers as their own, wavering and falling included. This curve, which depicts the responsibility of coming down instead of the thrill of going up, is the decisive point of emotional resonance with the primary target (late twenties to early thirties) — a hero who is deeply understood only when seen again as an adult. Kuro is the romantic hero of past shonen manga reshaped with a contemporary sensibility, and the character-level embodiment of the "newtro" this work pursues.

BNon-assimilating character handling — an ensemble that does not force a right answer.

This work does not patch up conflict with unconditional understanding, assimilation or a sense of comradeship among its characters. Throughout the work, a companion's decision, collapse or choice does not draw exaggerated, moved reactions out of the other characters. Each character stays in their own grain, and the work moves on. This is the result of the author recognizing and respecting the grain unique to each character, and it is the design that determines the emotional authenticity of the ensemble. The characters never quite unite in one heart and one mind; they keep "a precarious, narrow distance" and still do not scatter — this balance creates the work's emotional depth.

CMeta-awareness that accumulates and intensifies part by part — a newtro that keeps renewing itself.

The meta-awareness of this work is not a static code but an evolving curve that accumulates and intensifies part by part. It starts from the cliché-aware comedy of Part 1 (Herbaeken's "legend of the culture-festival band club"), and in Part 2 a character begins to trace the structure of the work itself — the passage where Herbaeken, facing "That Being" in the cathedral, cries out "A silver- or white-haired design, a nonhuman being who contemplates the multiverse or the timelines and has come to shake the protagonist's destiny, or else something like an apostle of God—!?" is an unmistakable meta utterance in which a character deduces the events of his own world from subculture clichés. In Part 3, the new nonhuman characters face the style of the existing cast from a distance — meta remarks along the lines of "What's with that cool-guy hairstyle that was in fashion back in '08~!? So tacky!" It means the work does not get trapped in its own style, and from a producer's point of view it is a decisive signal of the IP's longevity.

The nonhuman characters who appear from Part 3 onward each embody a different zeitgeist (from the 2010s to 2026 and beyond), so introducing one new character means adding a new timeliness and a new meta coordinate. As the parts progress, this IP does not weaken; it deepens. (For the extensibility of the part-based structure itself, see PART 07.)

As a result, this combination is neither familiar in advance nor predictable through comparative analysis. It is renewed with every part, its unpredictability accumulates, and the work faces its own style more and more deeply. In other words, as the parts progress, the work's core code — newtro, "the self-aware reconstruction of an old style" — does not fade but grows sharper, and the evolution of the meta-awareness itself works as the engine that renews the newtro. That a region not fully captured by analysis remains inside the work, and that even that region is absorbed into the evolution of newtro — this is the final thread that makes the work powerful as an IP.

PART 03

Target Readers

The producer's question: "Who reads this? Who is the one paying?" — They look at who buys it before whether it is good. Splitting the audience into primary, secondary and tertiary shows that the design runs from the "core buyers" through to the "audience it can expand into." Without this, it becomes "I can see it's good, but who do we sell it to?"
TierProfile
Primary targetLate twenties to early thirties. The generation that grew up watching Japanese anime of the 1990s–2010s, a thick layer found alike in Korea, the English-speaking world and the Chinese-speaking world. Responds immediately to the keyword "that feeling from back then."
Secondary targetLate teens to early twenties. Readers at home in a global J-culture that knows no nationality (anime, Vocaloid, J-pop). They have not seen the old anime, but are drawn in by the freshness of "I've never read anything with this texture."
Tertiary targetReaders at home with character IP and illustration. The cast's visuals are designed around beautiful characters and hold the eye very strongly. With appearances that differ before and after transformation, costume variations, and transformation signs such as masks and wigs, the work has strong potential for merchandise, art books, fan works and visually driven buzz on social media.
PART 04

Logline · Synopsis · Treatment

The producer's question: "Can this person write to the end? Does the story stick the landing?" — The box that proves the ability to finish by showing everything through to the ending. It is the section producers spend the longest on. Unlike material for readers, every spoiler has to be laid bare.

Logline

An ordinary high school boy who carries the power of a Black Dragon capable of destroying the world fights to protect his everyday life — a school fantasy.

Full Synopsis (four-act structure, spoilers included)

※ This synopsis is a plot summary based on Parts 1 and 2 (24 episodes in total), which have been fully written to date. The work as a whole is designed as at least four parts; for the direction and expansion structure of Parts 3 and 4, see PART 07 (Release and Story Structure).

[Introduction — Part 1 (Rise of the Dragon), Eps. 1–6: The Dragon's Revival and Everyday Life]

On his first day at St. Saints Academy, model student Ryuo Kuro has his blood targeted by Ferel, the vampire student council president, and in that crisis the legendary Black Dragon Lackness, asleep inside him, awakens. His childhood friend Eunho hides this fact and protects Kuro, while the exchange student Herbaeken, behind the mask of a cheerful legend otaku, watches for his chance to execute Lackness. Kuro goes through an incident every episode — a marionette principal gone mad (Shota), a sacred beast of an abandoned holy site (Lubell), a dark-mage teacher (Murasaki) — and yet does not remember that he himself turns into Lackness. Through the succession conflict with his cousin Miko and the appearance of the Fox Spirit, Kuro finally becomes aware of his dragon-clan blood and of the power within him.

[Development — Part 1 (Rise of the Dragon), Eps. 7–12: Crossed Fates and the First Cracks]

Herbaeken shows his true colors, and the two confront each other directly in the "Exhibition Hall of Condemnation," where he has confined Lackness. Lackness, who has no memory of his previous life, is shaken for the first time before the record of the ruin he brought about. Amid school events such as the school trip and the culture festival, Kuro's twin brother Shiro (= Lumensia) appears, declares that "Ore-sama is the strongest in the world," and turns the school into a tournament arena. Part 1 closes with the showdown between Lackness and Lumensia, and Herbaeken breaks with his family and becomes a companion to Kuro and Eunho.

[Turn — Part 2 (The Ryuo Clan Arc), Eps. 1–10: The Family's Sins and Ruin]

Now a second-year, Kuro faces the reality of the Ryuo family as his succession as head approaches. Miko and Eunho independently trace the family's dark history — a past in which the former head, Ren, colluded with the curse-working Kagehira family, had people killed and built up wealth. Kuro meets Aoi, the head of the Kagehira family, by chance and forms a bond with him without knowing who he is. Eunho and Miko infiltrate the Kagehira family and come face to face with Aoi, learning that the Kagehira family is a doctrinal group of thirty thousand people and that Aoi has made himself the gatekeeper of that hell. Aoi says, "If nothing of Ren's spirit remains in the head of the Ryuo family, then that's enough," and chooses forgiveness over revenge. Eunho at last tells Kuro all of the Ryuo family's sins, and on top of that the truth comes out that Kuro's parents were murdered from inside the family. Collapsing under the shock, Kuro fully accepts the power of Lackness and proclaims the very same "salvation through destruction" that destroyed a kingdom a thousand years ago.

[Conclusion — Part 2 (The Ryuo Clan Arc), Eps. 10–12: Return and Rebuilding]

Eunho stakes all the trust built over seventeen years and stands in the way of the rampaging Kuro. Faced with the gamble of "Get rid of me first," Kuro cannot attack him. Lackness's logic of ruin collapses before the "trust" Eunho planted, and Kuro returns to his original self. By his own decision, Kuro carries out the will that Yoshiyuki never managed to fulfill — taking the Kagehira family in as members of the family. The Ryuo family accepts the people of the Kagehira family, Miko returns to the Ryuo family and takes charge of its management, and Herbaeken joins the family as well. Kuro's true face, which had been hidden by Eunho's spell, is revealed, and he holds the Ryuo Family Revival Festival with the twin Dragon Gods as its mascots. Meanwhile Herbaeken is drawing closer to the truth of the dragon-clan legend through "That Being," whom he met in the cathedral, and the next season is foreshadowed as Lackness's eyes snap open.

Treatment

※ A treatment is a prose document that unfolds the synopsis (the compressed four acts) into a present-tense narrative to show the work's tone, scene transitions and the flow of its key beats. This treatment has a two-tier structure — ① Parts 1 and 2 are a formal treatment distilled from the completed text, and ② Parts 3 and 4, not yet written, are a planned outline that organizes the planning direction given in PART 05 and PART 07. It serves as the first reference for direction notes at the animation or webtoon stage. (Draft — subject to the author's review and revision)

Part 1 — Rise of the Dragon Based on the text · Formal

[Act 1] The Boy Who Wants to Be Ordinary Carries a Monster (Ep. 1)

An old-fashioned Japanese house where a shishi-odoshi sounds; the Ryuo family, keepers of a dragon shrine. Ryuo Kuro, a model student in glasses, prays before his parents' memorial portraits, leaves with an "ittekimasu," and heads to school. He wants only one thing: an ordinary high school life in which he does not stand out. St. Saints Academy, where he arrives amid the sly teasing of his childhood friend Eunho, is an eccentric school with a gothic exterior and a mix of Western and Japanese styles. At the entrance ceremony the beautiful vampire student council president Ferel, the doll-like principal Shota and the cheerful exchange student in the next seat, Herbaeken, appear one after another, and in front of the mural of the [Legend of the Dragon] Herbaeken points to Lackness, the strongest Black Dragon who destroyed the dragon clan, his eyes shining. Beside him, Eunho turns cold for a moment.

That day, in the student council's rose garden, at the moment Ferel, drunk on the smell of blood, is about to bite Kuro's neck — the Black Dragon Lackness, asleep for a thousand years, awakens for the first time, sets the garden ablaze and drives Ferel off. Eunho catches the collapsing Kuro in his arms — "I will protect you" — while Herbaeken, on the rooftop, looks down at the same face — "I will kill you." Two exactly opposite gazes fixed on the same boy: that is where Part 1 begins.

[Act 2] A Strange Tale Every Week, a Memory Erased Every Time (Eps. 2–4)

The academy spits out a strange tale every week, and at the center of each is always Kuro's kindness. Shota, the marionette principal made in place of a dead son, manipulates the whole student body to "gather the dragon" for a master who is already dead, and even Herbaeken is manipulated into attacking Kuro — but at his friend's voice in pain, Lackness awakens and grants Shota rest ("Doll who has lost its master. It is time you slept."). In the Legend Research Club that Herbaeken founds, Kuro takes in Lubell, a small beast cast out by its pack, holding it close even as it bites his hand. Murasaki, the dark-mage teacher who repays kindness with obsession and tries to trap Kuro as a first-year forever, is punished by Lackness once Kuro sees through that cowardice and is revolted by it.

The grammar these incidents share — every time, Kuro does not remember that he turned into Lackness. Because he refuses to hurt anyone, the power awakens in his place, settles things and vanishes. The memories of the whole student body are erased too, and only Eunho remembers everything.

[Act 3] The Night He Learns His Bloodline (Eps. 5–6)

His cousin Miko challenges Lackness to a duel with the succession as head at stake. Miko, once the disciple of Yoshiyuki (Kuro's father), unleashes shikigami and talismans but is powerless before the flames, and Lackness, defending without attacking, lets slip a decisive truth — the reason Kuro does not remember is that he is rejecting the power of his own will. For Miko, who has prided herself on being above Kuro in every respect, the reality that the seat is decided not by qualification but by "the power of the dragon" felt like a denial of herself. Defeated, Miko turns away hating Kuro to the last — the boy who "hates fighting that much" — but Kuro, without a trace of resentment toward the opponent who was cornering him a moment ago, sees her off with encouragement: "You're amazing, Miko, so you can do anything." Hatred and goodwill pass each other by without ever overlapping — this is the non-assimilating handling of relationships peculiar to this work. Eunho makes up his mind: "I have to tell him everything."

On the night of the summer festival, Kuro, left alone in the crowd, comes face to face in front of an abandoned shrine with a Fox Spirit whose face is the very image of Eunho's. The spirit calls himself a close friend of Yoshiyuki's and tells him the truth of the dragon clan — the dragon clan born as power and then mingled among humans, the Black Dragon who destroyed a kingdom, and the fact that the seal Yoshiyuki placed on the newborn Kuro has reached its limit. Kuro feels a strange flutter toward the spirit who tells him "Trust me," and the spirit finally tells him of the existence of his twin brother "Shiro," who refused the seal and vanished. The reader knows this spirit is Eunho. In the irony of Kuro devotedly following "the Fox Spirit" while grumbling at Eunho, who is the spirit himself, Eunho swallows a tangle of feelings, unsure whether to laugh or cry — the place of a spirit who dwells through faith and yet cannot fully receive that faith under his own name.

[Act 4] Those Who Walk Side by Side, at Cross Purposes (Eps. 7–8)

Herbaeken, raised under his family's indoctrination to kill the dragon, reveals who he is. He lures Lackness alone into the "Exhibition Hall of Condemnation" he spent the whole summer vacation preparing and presses him on the sin of the massacre a thousand years ago — but Lackness, who has no memory of his previous life, wavers for the first time, says that if it is true he apologizes deeply, and courteously bows his head. That contradiction, in which the legend that should have been evil is overturned, shakes Herbaeken. Eunho bursts in and the execution falls through, and the confrontation between the one who would protect and the one who would kill begins in earnest.

On the school trip, too, the two aim at each other from behind the mask of youth, and Herbaeken, faced with the kindness of the one he is supposed to kill, feels fear without even knowing that it is friendship. And that night, a boy who is the very image of Kuro, looking down on the lodgings — the twin, Shiro, shows himself.

[Act 5] The Pecking Order of Two Dragons, and the Hand That Stops Them (Eps. 9–12)

The culture festival. Exactly as Herbaeken recites the Japanese-anime legend that "the protagonist takes the stage in place of the band club that had an accident before the show," Lackness goes up on stage and sings the theme song. Into the middle of it bursts another dragon — the White Dragon Lumensia inside Shiro. Declaring that he "will become the strongest in the world," he joins the new principal, Shitsujide Hakase, in turning the academy into the stage of a "Strongest Tournament" and lords it over everyone as he pleases. In the rooftop final, contrary to everyone's expectation, Lackness tells his brother, "That's a nuisance (meiwaku)" — a scolding far too elementary for the Black Dragon who destroyed a kingdom.

This one line reveals the core of the work. Lackness is not a monster; he is the one who, in Kuro's place, protects the things Kuro cherishes. Even toward his brother Lumensia, who styles himself the strongest in the world, what he delivers is not annihilation but the rebuke "That's a nuisance" — not a destroyer but one who takes responsibility. Eunho sees that the difference in power between the two is not great, and that the newborn Lumensia simply cannot win because Lackness is "a power that has fought for a long time," reborn again and again. Hakase's gang throws the defeated Lumensia away like a worn-out shoe, and at the moment Lackness is about to deliver the final blow — Kuro stops him from within. "Don't kill him." Lackness warns that it is a dangerous judgment and yet yields. Back as Kuro, he holds out his hand to Shiro — who believes that without power he is useless and that getting along is something only weak fools do, a child who never once received an adult's care and learned that power alone was his worth — and in the end brings him home to the Ryuo family. Part 1 closes in a place that looks like a return to ordinary life, but around the same boy, the one who protects him, the one who meant to kill him and the newly joined brother begin to draw a precarious circle, and Herbaeken joins hands with Eunho, resolved to find out to the very end "whether this dragon is truly evil."

Part 2 — The Ryuo Clan Arc Based on the text · Formal

[Act 1] The Weight of the Head, the Shadow of the Family (Eps. 1–2)

A succession ceremony awaits Kuro, now a second-year. His wish not to become head is ignored, and he is made to sit in the seat of honor. When an elderly relative cries, "Let us revive the Ryuo family with the Dragon God at our front, as in Ren's day," Miko coldly cuts him off — "There is no such thing in the Ryuo family anymore." After the ceremony, Miko lets Kuro glimpse the family's shadow for the first time. Behind the splendor of the previous head Ren, who was called "the benevolent head," lay corruption and a "faith business," and his father Yoshiyuki went through a near bankruptcy while cleaning it up. "Becoming the head means shouldering all of that past as well." The relatives' talk behind her back ("Good thing it's docile Kuro who's head") digs into Miko's pride. And late that night — a group with their faces hidden behind black cloth pay their respects at the shrine of the land god and move to this land, and "That Being" in the cathedral gives notice: from now on, the horizon of battle shifts from power to the mind.

A few days later, Eunho calls Miko out alone to a hamburger shop downtown. What Miko knew went only as far as the Ryuo family's corruption, but Eunho tells her the whole picture beyond it — that Ren built his wealth by handing believers and orphans over to the curse-working Kagehira family as sacrifices, that after he died by divine punishment the Kagehira family was abandoned, and that Kuro's father Yoshiyuki went to the Kagehira family to take responsibility for that sin and never came back. Miko senses it — Yoshiyuki's death may not have been an accident. When Eunho says, "Before long I'll tell Kuro everything, too," Miko cuts him short. "Don't tell Kuro." And she strikes Eunho where it hurts. "Do you seriously think Kuro is fit to be head?" — the beginning of the crack that will run between the two throughout Part 2.

[Act 2] Two Heads — Light and Shade (Eps. 3–4)

The light side — Kuro and Aoi. In front of Kuro, crushed by the weight of being head, a handsome young man stops to ask the way. A black kimono, loosely worn long hair, a dialect and an endearingly scatterbrained air — this is Kagehira Aoi. An adopted son who came from Korea, and a young man who himself had to take on the headship at fourteen. Simply because Aoi is a young head like himself, Kuro opens his heart as if he has gained someone on his side. As befits a family of sorcerers, Aoi calmly believes Kuro's outlandish circumstances (a dragon, a Fox Spirit, a family head). But that night, after they part, Aoi's gentle smile is put away. Walking into a room lined with curse dolls and offerings, he commands oni and carries out a commission — "The self that accepted despair in order to protect something."

The shade side — Eunho and Miko. Meanwhile, the confrontation between the two that began over hamburgers in Act 1 does not stop there but descends deeper. While Kuro gains, for the first time, someone on his side in a new friend, the two who have watched over him the longest clash with him left out. Miko presses, "To protect the Ryuo family as it is now, my strength is needed; Kuro is too kind and will only be pushed around by the adults," and Eunho explodes, "Don't treat Kuro like a fool — he's just holding it in." Then Miko's weak spot shows — "Do you, too, think I'm unqualified because I'm Ren's daughter?" At the bottom of Miko's ambition lay the wound of believing she would not be acknowledged unless she proved herself endlessly. And Miko stabs precisely at Eunho's blind spot — at the moment he spoke of the Kagehira matter to her first and not to Kuro, even Eunho had unconsciously sorted Kuro as "a child to be protected" and Miko as "an adult to share the responsibility with." Struck where it hurts, Eunho cannot argue back. Miko, saying she no longer wants to be pitied, chooses isolation. "I'm more than enough to lead the Ryuo family on my own. Even that sin — I'll shoulder all of it." Eunho swallows the words — "That... is not Yoshiyuki's will." Kuro, delighted to have gained an ally for the first time, and the two who break apart leaving him out in order to protect him. Kuro's "exclusion" sprouts in exactly this crossing. And at the end of that confrontation, Eunho and Miko sense the oni of the Kagehira family on the move — the signal that the buried past of the Ryuo family has at last begun to show itself.

[Act 3] The Light and Shade of One Man — Infiltration and Forgiveness (Eps. 5–9)

Here again two strands cross, with one person — Aoi — between them.

The light side — Kuro and Aoi. Kuro shows Aoi around the Ryuo family shrine. The shrine that enshrines the Dragon God — half of the town's commercial facilities still belong to the Ryuo family, yet the shrine at its center has no flashy photo spots and no character charms; its "outdated way of doing business" has left it deserted, a place where time has stopped. Kuro confesses, with guilt in his voice: "The Dragon God is only strong. He can't grant a single wish." Aoi soothes him — "If such a cute and kind Dragon God is there to welcome them, that alone is comfort enough." And at the moment the wind blows, Aoi asks with a sidelong glance — "Would the Dragon God welcome a murderer, too?" Kuro senses that this person carries a weight he cannot speak of. And he answers — the Dragon God himself is a sinner who annihilated his people a thousand years ago, so he could hardly reproach someone who has committed the same sin. In a voice that trembles as if it were his own doing, though the sin is not his. Without even knowing it, Kuro makes room even for a murderer.

The shade side — Eunho and Miko. At the same hour, the two follow the oni and infiltrate the Kagehira family. After breaking through a crowd in black mourning clothes and the fierce resistance of Yuka, a girl their own age, they face the head, Aoi — the very young man who was with Kuro that afternoon. The truth Aoi tells is heavy. The abandoned Kagehira family returned to its original way of carrying out curses at the cost of its members' own lives, and has now become a vast group of thirty thousand people drawn together not by blood but by blind doctrine. The Kagehira bloodline has already died out, and even Aoi is an adopted son who was brought in — a man who has taken it upon himself to remain the gatekeeper of this hell until he dies. Though he has every justification for revenge, Aoi chooses forgiveness. "If nothing of Ren's spirit remains in the head who succeeds Yoshiyuki, then that's enough." Without any of the three knowing it, the acceptance Kuro gave his friend and the forgiveness Aoi gave the Ryuo family overlap — over the same person, at the same time.

In front of him, Miko's ambition collapses. Aoi dissects Miko precisely — what lies at the end of "having to become head" in order to shake off her father's guilt and be acknowledged? The blade of the very question she threw at Eunho in Act 1 now comes back to Miko herself through Aoi's mouth.

Herbaeken's thread runs alongside. Shaken by the contradiction of Lackness — whom he had believed to be nothing but evil — apologizing with a bowed head, he is now drawn not by revenge but by "the truth" itself. "That Being" in the cathedral tells him of the origin and fall of the dragon clan — the story of how the dragon clan, having descended on a land of death and been worshipped as "gods," came to mistake themselves for gods, lost their power, and perished together with their kingdom.

[Act 4] The Boy Who Breaks — Salvation Through Destruction (Ep. 10)

At last Eunho tells Kuro everything about the sins of the Ryuo family and the existence of the Kagehira family. Kuro holds out a naive hope — "We have to help them, we have to get them out" — but Eunho no longer veils the truth out of consideration and breaks that hope piece by piece. The despair of Lackness a thousand years ago, who with the power left to him could save no one, overlaps with the Kuro of now. Only then does Kuro fit together every piece of the puzzle that was Aoi — why he wandered praying to the land gods, why he said he had thirty thousand younger siblings, why he asked whether there was a god who would accept even a murderer.

On top of that, the decisive blow falls. As the family is being pressed for answers, Jiji's tongue slips, and the truth that Kuro's parents were murdered by none other than hands within the family comes out. Where the grandfather he had tried so hard to revere and the relatives who had seemed merely "ordinary" collapse in the worst possible form, Kuro's mind cannot hold and resonates with Lackness more intensely than ever. Accepting at last the power he had denied and pushed away, Kuro proclaims exactly what was proclaimed a thousand years ago. "Destruction alone is the one salvation that can deliver the world from hell. My mercy is to burn this world to the last and grant it the repose of nothingness (無)." The work portrays this savior's impulse not as a hero's awakening but as the most dangerous catastrophe.

[Act 5] Get Rid of Me First — The One Who Came Down Pulls Him Down (Ep. 10)

Eunho stands in the way of the rampaging Lackness. Before the flames, the Fox Spirit's identity is finally revealed, and Lackness wavers — "You deceived me. Even you." That very tremor is the proof that Kuro is alive within. Eunho stakes seventeen years of trust in one piece. "I came down from the seat of the gods (神の座) and protected you in the same form as you." And he walks into the blood-red flames and makes his gamble — "If you want to kill everyone that badly, get rid of me first."

Lackness's hand rises toward Eunho and stops in midair. The trust planted in Kuro's heart over seventeen years has stopped that hand. "What have you done to me?" Lackness asks, and Eunho answers: neither the nen (念) engraved on this land nor your own heart may be trampled so carelessly. Power must not be accepted in that way. And with the same hand that first came down from the seat of the gods, this time he pulls Kuro down from his lonely throne. "Come down from there, Kuro." The flames go out, and all that remains there, collapsed, is one wounded boy.

[Act 6] Return and Rebuilding — Yoshiyuki's Unfinished Will (Eps. 11–12)

When he wakes, Kuro accepts that Lackness is a part of himself he had been denying, and decides the path he must take. By his own decision he completes the last will that his father Yoshiyuki never managed to take responsibility for — embracing the Kagehira family as members of the family. The Ryuo family takes in the people of the Kagehira family and breaks down the family's boundaries, and Herbaeken joins them as well. Yuka, who once pointed a blade at her, follows Miko around like a chick.

Miko's arc lands here too. Before Yoshiyuki's grave, Miko weakly admits that she has "become a disciple walking a different path from her teacher," and that she may no longer even remember Yoshiyuki — at the end of the compulsion to prove herself that began in Act 1, she finally faces what her wound really is. Beside her, Eunho holds out his hand. "Come back to the Ryuo family."

The Kagehira family seeps into every corner of the Ryuo family to find whoever was behind Yoshiyuki's killing, and Kuro's true face, which had been veiled by Eunho's spell, is revealed. In May, a Revival Festival is held in front of the Ryuo family shrine with the "twin Dragon Gods" as its mascots. Not "a god to be held in awe" but "a friendly guardian deity" — friendly, yet showing majesty at the finish: a new branding that is named outright in the story as a "gap moe strategy." From an ordinary student to a head who takes on the family's sins and karma — outwardly a promotion, but in truth a curve of descent into a heavier burden — is completed here. And behind the festival, "That Being," contemplating the Ryuo family, murmurs toward Lackness — "If you have decided what to believe in and what to fight for, let us proceed to the next play. Once more, to take hold of wagahai (眞ノ鍵)." (Wagahai is the first-person pronoun with which he refers to himself, written "眞ノ鍵 (Shin no Kagi).") At the same hour, Herbaeken takes one step closer to the torn picture of the dragon-clan legend. In the final cut, Lackness, who had been lying in bed, snaps his eyes open, foreshadowing the next season.

Parts 3 & 4 — Planned Outline Not yet written · Planning direction

※ The following covers sections that have not yet been written; it is a "planned" outline that puts the planning direction stated in PART 05 (World) and PART 07 (Story Structure) into treatment form. Unlike the formal treatment of Parts 1 and 2, it is at the design stage with no text behind it, and may change when it is actually written.

[Part 3 — Planned] The Nonhumans Step Forward (12 episodes planned · Tone: lightness regained)

If Parts 1 and 2 were the stage of family politics in the human world (the Ryuo family, the Kagehira family and the other underworld families), in Part 3 the nonhuman (人外) realm that has existed behind it all along comes to the fore. The tone loosens again into the lightness of Part 1, making this a second gateway for new readers. Allied nonhumans who formed ties with the dragon clan in the past, and enemy nonhumans with layered motives — invading the human world, or going after "That Being," the one who calls himself Shin no Kagi (眞ノ鍵) — newly appear and are built up. Each of these new nonhumans embodies a different zeitgeist (from the 2010s to the present) and faces the style of the existing cast from a distance — meta remarks such as "What's with that cool hairstyle that was in fashion back in '08? So tacky!" show that the work does not get trapped in its own style. In the meantime, the pursuit of the truth by Herbaeken and "That Being" begins in earnest, moving step by step toward the torn picture of the dragon-clan legend (a picture, with part of it missing, of the dragon clan holding something aloft).

[Part 4 — Planned] The Payoff of the Legend — Full-Scale Fantasy (12 episodes planned · Tone: grand)

Part 4 graduates from the shell of a school story and shifts to full-scale fantasy and nonhuman battles. The mysteries accumulated in Parts 1 and 2 — Lackness's previous life, the "power of the gods (the power of natural disaster)" that the dragon clan lost and why it vanished, the identity of what the dragon clan was holding aloft in the torn picture, and the identity of Shin no Kagi (眞ノ鍵) — are paid off all at once, and the work's core keyword, "legend," comes to the fore. It is the stretch in which a story that began as a family drama in the human world finally expands to the scale of the world.

PART 05

World and Setting

The producer's question: "Is this world wide enough to carry n parts? Are there contradictions in the setting?" — If the synopsis is "what happens," the world is "why it can happen." Only when the political structure among the families, the system of sorcery and so on are solid does the judgment come: "This can yield a Part 3 and a Part 4 as well."

The dragon clan and the Ryuo family (龍王家): A thousand years ago, dragons that descended from the sky blessed a barren land, were worshipped as "gods" and founded a kingdom; after the kingdom fell, they mingled among humans and forgot even that they were the dragon clan. The cause and effect of that fall is peeled away in two stages within the work — the legend says that the strongest Black Dragon, Lackness, destroyed the kingdom, and in Part 1 Herbaeken believes this and moves to execute Lackness. In Part 2 the cause behind it comes out: the dragon clan, mistaking themselves for gods, lost the "power of the gods," and Lackness, watching the land turn into hell once the blessings ceased, finally despaired and burned everything as "salvation through destruction." The legend was right about the facts; what had been erased was the motive. This structure creates the emotional peak of the latter half of Part 2 — because Kuro, having learned of the family's sins and his parents' deaths, arrives at exactly the same logic as a thousand years ago and is about to repeat the same choice. The Ryuo family carries the blood of that dragon clan, and after a thousand years the power of Lackness manifests in it. Outwardly it is a traditional family running a shrine, hot springs and restaurants, but in the era of the previous head Ren there was corruption disguised as charity and collusion with the Kagehira family, and Yoshiyuki was murdered while cleaning it up. At present it is being rebuilt in Kuro's generation. The headship passed from Jiji to his two sons, Ren and then Yoshiyuki, but both died and Jiji took it up again; in Part 2 Kuro inherits it.

St. Saints Academy: An eccentric school with a gothic exterior and a mix of Western and Japanese styles. Founded in '08. Murals of the dragon legend line its corridors, an abandoned holy site lies behind it, and secret passages run beneath it. Vampires, marionettes, magical beasts and the like exist there, tied to the academy's history in the form of the "Seven Great Legends."

The Kagehira family (影片家): An underworld family that uses forbidden arts (禁術). A group of sorcerers who carry out curses at the cost of their own lives. It grew huge through collusion with Ren of the Ryuo family, but was abandoned after Ren's death and degenerated into a doctrinal group of thirty thousand people. The current head, Aoi, is an adopted son with no blood ties, and has made himself the gatekeeper of this hell. Though not of the bloodline, he pays with his own lifespan every time he performs the forbidden arts, so he too stands under the fate of a short life.

Political tension among the families: The Ryuo family cut its ties with the underworld after Ren's death, but remnants remain of the organization in which several families once colluded around the Ryuo family. The Kagehira family has kept a strict line between itself and other families and worked on an independent commission basis, but in joining the Ryuo family it breaks down its boundary with the outside for the first time. This move is spreading among the underworld families as a rumor that "the Ryuo family is being used by the Kagehira family."

Reference · Worldmap (planned)
The nonhuman world of Parts 3 & 4 — shown in silhouette
(the nonhuman realm expanding beyond the human world)

Parts 3 & 4 — expansion into the nonhuman (人外) world: If Parts 1 and 2 were the stage of family politics in the human world (the Ryuo family, the Kagehira family and the other underworld families), in Parts 3 and 4 the nonhuman realm that has existed behind it all along comes to the fore. Allied nonhumans, who are friendly to the human world or formed ties with the dragon clan in the past, and enemy nonhumans with layered motives, who move to invade the human world or to get hold of "That Being," the one who calls himself Shin no Kagi (眞ノ鍵), newly appear, and through them the scale of the world and its conflicts are renewed and expanded with each part.

PART 06

Main Character Profiles

The producer's question: "Will these characters sell merchandise? Will a fandom form? Can their stories run for n parts?" — The view that sees characters as marketable assets. Why "lack" is written down: a lack creates a growth arc, and a growth arc makes long-term serialization possible.
Character Design
Consistent Use of the Gap Structure

Almost without exception, the main characters of this work are designed around a deliberate gap between appearance and essence — the formula for character appeal proven in the Japanese market as "gap moe (ギャップ萌え)." An ordinary model student ↔ the world's strongest Black Dragon (Kuro); an innocent little brother ↔ the "ore-sama" White Dragon (Shiro); a beautiful student council president ↔ a vampire (Ferel); a cute child ↔ a marionette (Principal Shota); a cheerful otaku ↔ an avenger (Herbaeken); a sly childhood friend ↔ a Fox Spirit (Eunho); a laid-back Kansai dialect ↔ the gatekeeper who shoulders a hell of thirty thousand people (Aoi). In the Ryuo Family Revival Festival scene, this gap structure is named outright within the story — "Friendly, yet showing the majesty of the Dragon God at the finish: in a word, a gap moe strategy." It is further proof of the work's self-awareness — it knows what it is doing as it does it — and a device that decisively raises how readily the characters are taken up in the Japanese market.

Everyday
Awakened
Ryuo Kuro (protagonist)
Head of the Ryuo family · Host of the Black Dragon Lackness

Personality: Timid and faint-hearted, but good-natured. As much as he hates fighting, he treasures his relationships.

Arc: In Part 1 he rejects the power and is swept along by it; in Part 2 he learns of the family's sins, breaks once, then accepts the power and stands as head — a growth arc. Not an overpowered hero, but a character who agonizes over "how to use power once you have it."

Lack: The absence of his parents; a lack of self-assurance. "I'm not amazing like Dad."

Everyday
True form
Eunho (childhood friend / Fox Spirit)
Kuro's guardian · Trickster

Personality: Looks sly and frivolous, but is absolutely serious in anything that concerns Kuro.

Arc: Seventeen years lived as a human boy at Kuro's side, at Yoshiyuki's request. His identity is exposed in Part 2, Episode 10, and with a gamble that stakes those seventeen years of trust he brings the rampaging Kuro back.

Lack: The contradiction of being a spirit and yet bound by human feelings. The weight of the choice of having "come down from the seat of the gods."

Everyday
Hidden side
Herbaeken (German exchange student / descendant of the people of the fallen kingdom)
An antagonist who infiltrated to execute Lackness · Kuro's friend

Personality: On the surface a cheerful legend otaku; underneath, a cursed boy possessed by revenge.

Arc: Hesitates over the trigger between revenge and friendship, and in the end breaks with his family and stands on his friends' side. From Part 2 onward he comes into his own as a legend otaku, pursuing the truth of the legend through "That Being" in the cathedral.

Lack: A thirst for revenge instilled by his family's indoctrination. Unfamiliarity with the very feeling of friendship.

Everyday
Awakened
Ryuo Shiro (twin brother)
Kuro's twin · Host of the White Dragon Lumensia

Personality: The grain of an innocent, curious small child. His expression of feeling is frank and direct, and his reaction to praise is explosive. With a short sense of social distance, he enjoys everything as if seeing it for the first time.

Arc: The other dragon-clan twin, who refused Yoshiyuki's seal and vanished. He first shows himself to Kuro at the academy's culture festival in Part 1, Episode 9, and the White Dragon Lumensia within him confronts his elder brother Lackness head-on. At the end of Part 1 the relationship between the two brothers is settled, and he finds his place as someone who stays at Kuro's side.

Lack: A being who refused the seal and had disappeared. The very fact that he has kept the grain of a small child, never having received an adult's care, is the form his lack takes.

Ryuo Miko (cousin / rival for the headship)
Ren's daughter · Yoshiyuki's disciple · the Ryuo family's management elite

Personality: Sky-high pride and a fierce temper, but the root of that fierceness is a need to make up for her father's sins.

Arc: In Part 2 Aoi strikes her where it hurts — "the father's blood runs thicker than the teacher's" — and she breaks for a time, but she later returns to the Ryuo family and leads its management.

Lack: The stigma of being "Ren's daughter." A hunger for recognition.

Kagehira Aoi (head of the Kagehira family)
Head of an underworld family · to Kuro, "a young head like me"

Personality: On the surface a laid-back, playful speaker of Kansai dialect, and a young man whose true nature is kindness. As the head who shoulders thirty thousand lives, however, he has no choice but to be ruthless.

Arc: Chooses forgiveness over revenge and accepts joining the Ryuo family. Bearing the fate of a short life, his lifespan shaved away each time he uses the forbidden arts, he is a man who has nailed himself to this place, unable to leave it until he dies.

Lack: The karma of a family he took on without blood ties. "I have to stay here until I die."

PART 07

Release and Story Structure — The Author's Own Pacing

The producer's question: "What is the release cycle? Is the structure able to hold readers without drop-off?" — They look at whether each weekly episode is built to hold the reader. A distinctive rhythm like BHS's "Part 1 episodic → Part 2 serial" becomes a selling point here. Added value such as suitability for short-form marketing belongs here too.
PartSubtitleEpisodesToneStructure
Part 1Rise of the Dragon12 episodesLight, comedic, episodicA case solved in every episode. Self-contained episodes build up the characters.
Part 2The Ryuo Clan Arc12 episodesHeavy, dramatic, serialThe first turning point of the protagonist's growth arc. Feature-film pacing centered on psychological warfare and relationship drama.
Part 3TBD12 episodesLight, comedicBuild-up of new nonhuman characters (allies and enemies).
Part 4TBD12 episodesGrand, full-scale fantasyNonhuman battles. The full payoff of the accumulated legend mysteries.

Release Format — Whole Parts Released at Once (the Seasonal Streaming Model)

This work was planned on the premise of releasing all 12 episodes of a part at once rather than weekly episode-by-episode serialization. A whole part opens at the same time, and readers finish it at their own pace. It brings to the web novel the rhythm of a seasonal streaming service rather than a pay-per-episode model.

  • Preventing drop-off — Low dependence on episode-end cliffhangers, and character build-up is never cut off midway. The feeling of "I can't wait until the next episode" does not arise, so the completion rate after entry is high.
  • Igniting marketing and media mix at once — At the single moment of "Part n is out!", buzz, social media and press exposure explode, and media-mix lines such as print volumes, art books, merchandise and soundtracks can launch together. Unlike weekly serialization spread over twelve weeks, the release of a part is itself both the event and the package launch date.
  • Preserving the author's pacing — Tone shifts, the payoff of foreshadowing and the emotional curve reach the reader exactly as designed, without the arbitrary cuts of weekly serialization.
  • A differentiated revenue structure — A part-based package or subscription model can be applied instead of pay-per-episode. For platforms it can also be used as a new product slot. In English-language web fiction in particular, free serialization, early access for supporters (Patreon) and e-book sales by volume are the norm, so release by part actually fits naturally with market practice.

Differentiation by Part — A Different Kind of Fun Promised Each Time

The core strategy of this work is that each part has a clearly different focus and a different code of fun. Rather than repeating the same structure, it branches its genre grain with each part within a single work, continually creating new points of entry.

Part 1 (Rise of the Dragon) — Episodic Comedy / Entry Through Characters

The characters are built up through a self-contained case in each episode. A light tone and self-aware comedy lower the entry barrier, while a serious scene planted at the end of each episode lays the groundwork for the tone shift.

Because each episode is self-contained, the part is optimized for short-form marketing. It can be cut immediately into one- to two-minute excerpt videos or webtoon clips, and spreading on social media clip by clip comes naturally.

Part 2 (The Ryuo Clan Arc) — Drama and Psychological Warfare / The First Turning Point of Growth

This is the first turning point at which the protagonist's growth arc finally starts to move. The characters and world are the same as in Part 1, but tone and structure change markedly, shifting to a serial drama with feature-film pacing centered on the family's sins, psychological warfare between characters and fractures of identity.

Psychological warfare and relationship drama have a very high affinity with fan works, interpretive content and review videos. They drive content the fandom produces on its own — analyses of character motives, relationship charts, compilations of famous scenes — and generate buzz that travels outside the work. For readers who entered through Part 1, this part proves the work's emotional depth and forges the fandom's cohesion.

Parts 3 & 4 — The Extensibility of the Keyword "Legend"

Parts 3 and 4 are the stretch that pays off and expands the work's core keyword, "legend," in earnest. The mysteries accumulated in Parts 1 and 2 — why the dragon clan lost the "power of the gods," Lackness's previous life, the missing part of the torn picture, the identity of Shin no Kagi — are all designed as the driving force of Part 3 and beyond, and "legend," an abstract and extensible keyword, is an IP asset that can be expanded without limit. (For how the new characters of each part embody a zeitgeist and evolve the work's meta-awareness itself, see the detailed points of appeal in PART 02.)

  • A new character slot opens with every part — New nonhuman characters (allies and enemies) appear in each part. The lines of merchandise, illustration and fan works are recharged at regular intervals.
  • Diversified points of entry for readers — Because the tone lightens again in Part 3, a second gateway opens for new readers. A structure in which "you can start from Part n."
  • Extensibility made visible — The full-scale fantasy tone of Part 4 is the first step of a world that has graduated from the shell of a school story. It becomes the soil for IP expansion from Part 5 onward — side stories, spin-offs, new works branching off.

Each part has its own self-contained fun while the momentum toward the next part accumulates, and releasing whole parts at once delivers the completeness of this design to readers as it is.

PART 08

Comparable IP (Comp Titles)

The producer's question: "Are there similar success stories? Is this a line the market has proven?" — Not direct comparisons, but the box that shows "existing IP this readership is likely to enjoy." Material that lets a producer explain it upstairs in one line: "It's in the Black Butler + Naruto line."

Comparable IP — A Set of Partial Coordinates

This work is designed as a combination of "newtro + the texture of Japanese school anime + family dark fantasy + a multilingual web-novel format," so there is in effect no earlier comparable title that matches on every axis at once. This fact cuts both ways.

Two Sides
  • Weakness — It cannot be placed exactly on a hit line the market has already proven. The one-line formula "OO meets OO" does not come out cleanly.
  • Strength — The coordinates themselves are empty, so they can be claimed first. The work's identity itself can settle in as the market's "only coordinates."

Partially Matching Coordinates

Each comparable IP overlaps with this work in one or two dimensions, but none occupies the coordinates of this work, in which all the dimensions are combined. Below is a set of coordinates a producer can use when reporting upstairs, in the form "This work has side X of OO and side Y of OO."

TitleOverlapping coordinates
Black Butler (Yana Toboso)The combination of stylized beauty + comedic rhythm + family tragedy
Gintama (Hideaki Sorachi)Self-aware use of genre clichés. Wide tone shifts between light and serious. The emotional grain of a large ensemble cast
NarutoThe structure of a family's karma and the next generation overcoming it
Blue ExorcistThe line of school story + nonhuman battles + beautiful characters. Connects with the tone of Parts 3 and 4
⭐ Key Appeal
"It feels like something I've seen somewhere, but I can't recall where."

The comparable IPs in the table above overlap with some of this work's coordinates, but there is no single original that comes straight to mind on seeing this work. Familiar elements of old Japanese anime are melted throughout the work, yet it is built so that it cannot be reduced to direct borrowing from, homage to, or a knockoff (パクリ) of any one title.

That no one-line label of "This is like OO" sticks to it, and that it is not trapped in the shadow of a single IP — this is the most essential strength, the one that makes people recognize it as a work in its own right.

PART 09

Expansion and Publication Plan

The producer's question: "What else can be done besides the web novel? What about secondary revenue?" — The box that shows how many revenue lines come out of one IP: print volumes, merchandise, anime, webtoon and so on. "Each episode has already been converted to 10 minutes of running time" is evidence that the cost of converting it into anime is low.

IP Gateway Strategy — Why the Web Novel Was Chosen as the First Medium

This work was designed as an IP project rather than for a single medium, and the web novel was chosen as its first gateway. That is because the web novel is a medium in which ① a work can be built quickly and ② at the same time the author can accumulate visual references (character sheets and concept art) alongside it. As a result, the text and the visual references are completed together, making it an IP package that already contains within it the direction assets professional contractors will receive at the animation or webtoon stage.

🎯 PRIORITY STRATEGY
English First · Global Entry

This work takes the English-speaking world as its primary market, because a single language, English, reaches readers in many countries at once. The Chinese-speaking world, with its huge "erciyuan" (二次元) fandom, is the next key market, with Japan and Korea operated alongside, and the multilingual infrastructure for this is already up and running on the author's own site (rrkt.biz).

Multilingual Operation — Infrastructure Already at Work

  • Korean–Japanese hybrid edition — The original form of the work, with the Japanese borrowings fully alive. The original of the work's identity.
  • Pure Korean edition — A version for Korean readers with the Japanese borrowings removed for readability.
  • Japanese edition — Because the work's code takes direct aim at the grammar of Japanese anime in the first place, translation friction is small, and it is the most polished of the translated editions.
  • English–Japanese hybrid edition — The English counterpart of the Korean–Japanese hybrid edition. An edition for anime fans that melts romanized Japanese vocabulary into the English text and recreates the feel of the fansubs of that era.
  • Pure English edition — An edition for general English-language readers with the Japanese borrowings removed.
  • Chinese–Japanese hybrid edition (Simplified · Traditional) — The Chinese counterpart of the Korean–Japanese hybrid edition. An edition for anime fans that brings that era's feeling to life through the sound-rendered Japanese way of speaking familiar to Chinese-speaking anime fans.
  • Pure Chinese edition (Simplified · Traditional) — An edition for general Chinese-language readers with the Japanese borrowings removed. Both Simplified and Traditional can be read directly on the author's own site (the Simplified or Traditional interface and text open automatically according to the browser language), and collaboration with local platforms on serialization and licensing is envisaged as well.
  • Global accessibility — Other languages are immediately accessible through Google Translate, and the quality of the novel text is supplemented with DeepL translation.

In entering overseas markets, the cost of translation and localization and a mismatch of codes are usually the greatest barriers, but for this work ① the code itself is based on the grammar of Japanese anime, which knows no nationality, and ② the translation infrastructure has been built in advance, so the cost of entry is fundamentally low. In particular, in each of the three language areas of Korean, English and Chinese, a "hybrid edition" (Korean–Japanese, English–Japanese, Chinese–Japanese) and a "pure edition" (Pure Korean, Pure English, Pure Chinese) are placed side by side, so that anime fans and general readers can both be welcomed within a single original work.

Materials Provided by the Author (Asset Bundle)

The visual assets of this work are accumulated by the author personally in the form of character sheets and concept art. The author is a multi-disciplinary creator with experience in character design and illustration, but it is assumed that the visual production at the final animation or webtoon stage will be entrusted to professional illustrators and webtoon artists. The assets the author provides function not as final output in themselves but as reference and direction guides.

Materials Provided
  • 24 main episodes of the web novel + n side-story episodes (complete text)
  • Character sheets — made by the author / for reference
  • Visual references — made by the author / visualizing tone, costume and world
  • Concept art — made by the author / direction guide for professional contractors
What This Means for a Producer
  • Consistency of tone across contractors — The visual tone of the work is already clearly visualized in the mind of the author of the text, which shortens the time professional contractors need to pin down the tone.
  • Faster media-mix decisions — Producers and investors need not simulate in their heads "what this work would look like as an anime or webtoon"; they can judge at once from the author's references.
  • Coherence between core and visuals — Because text and visuals came from the same mind, the risk of tone drift across the whole IP is low.

Media It Can Expand Into (Expansion Lines)

Given how this work is designed, all of the following IP lines are possible. This does not mean every line must go at once; it means the range of options to choose from is wide.

Print Volumes

  • One volume per part. About 80,000 characters (Korean) per volume, designed as a collectible edition of 200 pages or fewer.
  • Once publication is confirmed, the plan is to produce a collectible edition with illustrations placed throughout the text.
  • Including illustrations, a setting book and side stories makes up for the page difference from an ordinary web-novel volume (a standard of 120,000 characters).

Character Merchandise · Art Books

  • The cast's visuals are designed around beautiful characters and hold the eye very strongly. (Rich in visual signs: appearances that differ before and after transformation, costume variations, masks, wigs and so on.)
  • High affinity with collectible products such as art books, acrylic stands and character goods.

Anime · Webtoon

  • The author has already converted each episode to 10 minutes of running time → low cost of conversion to anime or webtoon.
  • The storyboard-like prose direction functions as direction notes as it is.
  • Re-editing Part 1 as 12 episodes × 10 minutes = 120 minutes (the length of one feature film) is also possible.
  • Recently, more and more IP of Korean origin has been adapted into anime by way of production by Japanese studios with investment from global streaming platforms (e.g., Solo Leveling). This work, too, takes the English-speaking world as its primary market while being designed in the grammar of Japanese anime, so pairing it with production by a Japanese studio comes naturally.

Japanese-Style Media Mix (Live Action · Stage)

  • There is potential for the typical expansion lines of Japanese anime and manga IP — live-action film, 2.5D musicals and stage plays.
  • The work's stylized beauty, costumes and character visuals are already designed in a tone close to stage direction, so its suitability for the stage is higher than that of ordinary web novels.
PART 10

Risks and Responses

The producer's question: "Why might this fail? And how will you prevent that?" — Producers look at where things could blow up before the good points. A proposal that lists only strengths gives them nothing to judge by. Only when risks and responses are written together does a real review become possible.

Risk 1 — Accumulated Entry Barriers Across the Work

Japanese borrowings, many characters appearing at once, a multilayered family structure, a tone different from the web-novel mainstream — these can add up to a burden at first entry. The recommendation algorithms of Korean web-novel platforms are trained around regression, possession and overpowered-protagonist stories, and those of English-language web-fiction platforms around progression fantasy and LitRPG, so there is also the possibility that this work is pushed down in organic exposure.

→ Response: The code is the core of the work's identity, so it will not be weakened. Instead, in the multilingual infrastructure of PART 09, a hybrid edition and a pure edition are run side by side for each language area — the Korean–Japanese, English–Japanese and Chinese–Japanese hybrid editions are for anime fans who enjoy the Japanese code, and the Pure Korean, Pure English and Pure Chinese editions are for general readers, with the Japanese borrowings removed. The core of the work (characters, story, the newtro code) can be delivered intact even to readers who find the Japanese code a burden, and for Japanese readers there is the Japanese edition in their own language.

Risk 2 — The Two-Way Risk of Part 2's Tone Shift

The weight of Part 2 is a word-of-mouth weapon and at the same time carries a two-way risk.

  • (a) Readers who entered through the lightness of Part 1 may find the dramatic tone of Part 2 heavy and drop off.
  • (b) In Part 2 the work's reinterpretation of itself begins in earnest and the newtro coloring grows deeper, so it may come to be seen as a niche work.

→ Response: For (a), the serial foreshadowing in the latter half of Part 1 functions as advance notice and absorbs the shock of the tone shift. For (b), the four-part design, in which Part 3 loosens the tone back into the lightness of Part 1 and opens a new way in, acts as the buffer. The curve in which the tone changes with every part is itself the work's pacing design, and it is not trapped in any single tone.

Risk 3 — An Unproven Revenue Structure for Releasing Whole Parts at Once

The revenue model of Korean web novels is based on pay-per-episode serialization. Releasing whole parts at once has no proven revenue data in the Korean market, so domestic platforms may find it difficult to accept right away. (This is the operational risk of the seasonal streaming model presented in PART 07.)

→ Response: Release all at once is the ideal form that conveys the work's pacing most faithfully; it is not a mandatory condition. A hybrid model — serializing episode by episode and then re-releasing the part as a whole when it ends — can be applied. In addition, in the primary market, the English-speaking world, free serialization, early access for supporters and e-book sales by volume are the norm, so release by part is already a familiar structure.

Risk 4 — The Author's Burden in Multilingual Operation, and Translation Quality

At present the author alone runs seven editions — Korean–Japanese hybrid / Pure Korean / Japanese / English–Japanese hybrid / Pure English / Chinese–Japanese hybrid / Pure Chinese (the Chinese ones each in Simplified and Traditional) — plus an environment compatible with Google and DeepL translation. With so many editions, the risk of author burnout over a long serialization is high. Also, the author is not a native speaker of English, Japanese or Chinese, so if translation quality falls short of native level it could actually backfire in those markets. (This is the operating-cost side of the multilingual infrastructure presented in PART 09.)

→ Response: At the publication and licensing stage, switch to collaboration with native English, Japanese and Chinese translators and editors. In particular, for the English–Japanese and Chinese–Japanese hybrid editions the degree to which Japanese vocabulary is mixed in governs how the text feels to readers, and in the Chinese–Japanese hybrid edition sound-rendered expressions that carry unintended connotations must be filtered out, so native review is treated as essential. The author concentrates on writing the Korean–Japanese hybrid original, and the other editions are shared out by matching them with professional staff on the publisher's side. The author's own operation at present plays the role of "proof of infrastructure in advance," and at the stage of formal publication it can shift naturally to a professional division of labor.

PART 11

One-Line Pitch

This is not something the producer writes; it is the one line the producer takes upstairs. A service box designed so that when asked "What is this?", reading this one line aloud is all it takes. If it is posted on the author's homepage, it becomes the sentence by which visitors remember this proposal as they leave.
"The feel of the Japanese anime we remember, reconstructed with a contemporary sensibility — a newtro work to read in Korean–Japanese, English–Japanese and Chinese–Japanese hybrid editions."

ブラッドハイスクール(BHS) — IP企画書

作業用文書 · 各カテゴリーにその役割を説明するガイドを併記
※ 本企画書は、作者の資料と意図をもとに、AIアシスタント(Claude)との協業で作成されました。
FROM THE AUTHOR
作者のことば

はじめまして。『ブラッドハイスクール』を書いている作者です。

『ブラッドハイスクール』は、1990年代後半から2000年代、そして2010年頃までの日本の少年アニメの感性を、いまあらためて編み直したニュートロ学園ファンタジーです。企画書をお読みいただく前に、なぜ私がこの作品を作ることになったのかを、先にお話しさせてください。

私は十代の頃、その時期の日本のアニメを本当にたくさん観ました。あの頃のアニメは子どもっぽくて賑やかなのに、観ているうちにいつの間にか世界の暗い面と向き合わされる、哲学的でもあり、人間賛歌に近い物語がたくさんありました。主人公は優しくて正義感があり、乗り越えられない試練を前にしても人間らしさを守り抜く姿を、観る人たちが美徳として受けとめてくれてもいました。

けれども今は、そういう作品がもう一度生まれるのは難しいと感じています。あれは、あの時代だったからこそ成り立った感性だったからです。最近の作品は当時とは肌合いがずいぶん違いますし、私の感じるかぎりでは、優しくて正義感のある主人公が、以前ほど魅力的には描かれなくなったように思います。

だから私は、あの頃の作品で好きだったところを一つひとつ集めて、自分の手で作っています。ある意味では、『ブラッドハイスクール』は私自身のための作品でもあります。

そして、この作品を読んでくださる方々にも、あの頃のロマンを一緒に感じていただけたら嬉しいです。

ここから先は、この思いをもとに、作品の構造と可能性を整理した企画書です。

PART 01

作品基本情報

PDが最初に見るスペック表。「これは何? 何文字? どこまで出ている?」を3秒で判断する欄。ジャンル、分量、文字数で「うちのプラットフォーム/出版社に合う規格か」を確認する。ここで落ちると、下は読まれない。
項目内容
タイトルブラッドハイスクール (Blood High School · 略称 BHS)
ジャンル学園ファンタジー · ダークファンタジー · 成長 · 少年漫画
代表コードニュートロ (NEW-TRO)
版韓本語(原本) · 純韓国語 · 日本語 · 英本語 · 純英語 · 中本語 · 純中国語 (中国語は簡体字・繁体字)
IP路線Web小説(一次ゲート) → 単行本 / グッズ / 映像化 / ライセンシング
年齢区分15歳以上
構造シーズン制 (1シーズン12話 / 最低4部作を予定)
現在の進行2シーズン完結 — 第1部 龍の復活編 12話 + 第2部 竜王家編 12話
付加コンテンツ番外編 n編
1話あたりの文字数4,000〜5,000字 (韓国語原文基準)
1話あたりの体感分量アニメーション換算で約10分
1部あたりの総ランニングタイム約120分 (2時間)
PART 02

企画意図 — なぜこの作品なのか

PDの問い:「似たようなものはもうたくさんあるのに、なぜこれをやるべきなのか?」— この作品が市場のどの空席を占めるのかを説明する欄。作品の「面白さ」ではなく「ポジショニング」を見せる場所。「ニュートロ」という代表コードが、なぜ今この市場で機能するのかを解き明かす必要がある。同時に、この作品ならではの魅力ポイント(作者が狙ったポイント)をここに織り込んでもよい。ただし、「自分はこれが好きだ」ではなく、「これがなぜ市場で通用するのか」という言葉で書くこと。

作品の出発点

本作は、単純な渇きから出発しました。2000年代の日本アニメ特有の呼吸 — 軽さと重さを行き来する演出、あの頃のキャラクタービジュアル、クリシェを自覚的に運用する演出 — をもう一度味わいたいと願う読者層は、確かに存在します。しかし、同時代の韓国・英語圏・中国語圏のサブカルチャー市場のどこにも、その渇きを正面から狙った新作はほとんど見当たりません。作者はこの空席の最初の読者として、自分の渇きをそのまま市場の渇きに置き換え、本作を企画しました。

なぜ今、この市場で機能するのか

本作の「ニュートロ」は、単なる郷愁の再生ではなく、フィルタリングされた郷愁の再構成です。昔の作品ならではの情緒的な座標(呼吸、ビジュアル、クリシェの運用)はそのまま持ち込みつつ、同時代の感覚から見ると引っかかる要素(無条件の家族愛の強要、不必要な加虐性、間延びした物語)は意図的に取り除き、組み立て直しました。「あの頃のあの作品」をもう一度観たいのに、いざ開いてみると今の感覚に合わない部分が多くて最後まで行けない読者層 — 本作は、まさにその物足りなさを正確に埋めようとするものです。

このポジショニングは、韓国Web小説の主流(回帰・憑依・チート無双)とも、英語圏Web小説の主流(成長型ファンタジー・LitRPG — ゲームシステムが登場するファンタジー)とも直接競合せず、別の座標に位置します。

代表コード「ニュートロ」の定義

本作でいう「ニュートロ」とは、1990〜2008年(または〜2010年)頃の日本の少年アニメの文法を、現在の時点から自覚的に再構成するという意味です。

単なるレトロ感覚ではなく、あの時代のジャンル文法そのものを作動させるやり方です。

言語コードの意図的な混種性

本文はほぼ韓国語で書かれていますが、キャラクターの台詞やナレーションに、日本アニメ特有の語彙が自然に溶け込んでいます。「いってきます」「僕」「俺様」「静かに」「よろしく」「許さない」など(原文では、これらの日本語の音をハングルで表記しています)。読者はこのコードによって、即座に「あの頃のあのジャンル」という情緒的な座標に着地します。同時に、このコードを知らない読者にとっては「独特の文体」として機能し、差別化につながります。

さらに、この日本語コードは、キャラクターごとに話し方を区別する装置としても使われています。クロ(優等生: 標準語)、ウンホ(軽口の日本語スラング)、ミコ(お嬢様口調)、アオイ(関西弁) — 多人数の群像劇の中で、誰が話しているのかが話し方だけで即座に識別でき、日本語に翻訳した際にも、キャラクターごとのトーンの違いがそのまま生き残る構造です。

この装置は英語版でもそのまま機能します。英本語版は、英語の本文に同じ日本語の語彙をローマ字で溶け込ませた版で("Ittekimasu"、"Ore-sama"、"sensei" など)、2000年代の海外ファンが、翻訳されていない敬称や感嘆詞の混じったファンサブで日本アニメと初めて出会った体験を、文章で再現します。韓国・英語圏の読者にとっては、この混種文体そのものがあの頃の思い出になる、もう一つのニュートロ装置です。キャラクターごとの話し方の区別も、英本語版でそのまま保たれます。混種文体に馴染みのない読者のためには、日本語の借用を取り除いた純英語版を併せて用意しています。

中国語圏でも同じ装置が機能します。この言語圏のアニメファン文化(二次元)には、日本語を漢字の音に置き換えて混ぜる話し方(米娜桑・哦哈哟・卡哇伊 など)や、「~酱・~君・~様」のような敬称の借用が馴染み深く使われており、2000年代の字幕組(字幕组、ファンサブチーム)時代への郷愁も残っています。中本語版はこの話し方で書かれた版であり、一般読者向けの純中国語版も併せて用意しています。どちらの版も簡体字・繁体字で運用しています。繁体字版は、簡体字版の文字だけを置き換えたものではなく、繁体字を使う読者が読みやすいように、語彙と音訳表記まで別途整えた版です。

クリシェの自覚的な運用

ショタ校長、その校長より権力のある美貌の生徒会長、「俺様」ライバル、トーナメント、ツッコミとボケの構図、幼馴染=神霊、家門の悲劇 — いずれも「あの頃」の典型ですが、作品は自分がそれを使っていると知ったうえで使っています。「決めポーズ」のような自己認識型の描写、そして第9話でヘルバーケンが「文化祭にはバンド部の伝説があるんだ……バンド部は公演前に必ず事故に遭って、代打に主人公が登場する、日本アニメの伝説が……」と語るくだりが代表的です。クリシェを道具として使いながら、その事実を読者と共有するこの自覚性が、作品のコメディのリズムと情緒的な密度を同時に生み出しています。

ビジュアルコードの時代性

ニュートロの最も即効性のある入口は視覚です。読者は本文を開く前に、表紙・イラスト・キャラクターデザインの第一印象だけで、作品の時代性とジャンルを0秒で判断します。本作のキャラクタービジュアルは、2000年代の日本の学園・ダークファンタジーアニメが蓄積してきたビジュアル — ゴシック・様式美・誇張された髪のシルエット、コスチュームのバリエーションなど変身記号の豊かさ — を意図的に借用しています。これは参入障壁を最も速く越えさせると同時に、グッズ・イラスト集・二次創作といったIP拡張ラインでの認知をあらかじめ確保する、核となる装置でもあります。

VISUAL LOOKBOOK
文章より、絵で感じる
キャラクターの衣装 · 背景 · 色彩 — アニメ化に備えたビジュアルリファレンス
